--- Page 2 ---
<meta>Front flyleaf with pasted bookplate/emblem of the Bibliotheca Philosophica Hermetica (Ritman Library)</meta>

<insert>E</insert> <note>This letter "E" is likely a shelfmark or classification code used by the library to organize its collection.</note>

<note>This page features the bookplate of the Ritman Library (Bibliotheca Philosophica Hermetica), an institution dedicated to the study of Western Esotericism. The imagery is rich with <term>Hermetic</term> (spiritual and alchemical) symbolism:

*   **The Pelican in her Piety:** A mother pelican wounding her breast to feed her chicks with her own blood. In a museum context, this represents self-sacrifice and the "nourishment" of the soul or the alchemical process.
*   **The Stone Cube and Roses:** The pelican sits on a cubic stone adorned with four roses, a clear reference to the <term>Rosicrucian</term> tradition, which seeks a spiritual transformation of humanity.
*   **The Ouroboros:** The serpent biting its own tail forms a circle around the scene, a classic symbol for eternity, the cyclical nature of the universe, and the alchemical motto "All is One."
*   **The Radiant Sun:** Above the pelican, the sun represents divine light and the ultimate source of spiritual wisdom.</note>

->**HERMETIC PHILOSOPHY**<-
<note>original: "PHILOSÒPHIA • HERMETICA"</note>

<summary>This is the front flyleaf of the manuscript containing the official bookplate of the Ritman Library, featuring traditional symbols of alchemical and Rosicrucian philosophy.</summary>
<keywords>Hermetic Philosophy, Bibliotheca Philosophica Hermetica, Pelican in her piety, Ouroboros, Rose Cross, Alchemy, Ritman Library</keywords>

# To the High-born, Illustrious Lady,

->Madam<-

# AMELIA,

->By God’s grace, Princess of Orange, etc.<-

<note>Amelia van Solms (1602–1675) was the wife of Frederick Henry, Prince of Orange. She was a powerful and influential figure in the Dutch Republic, known for her political acumen and her role in the royal court at The Hague.</note>

HANNAH, the mother of Samuel, testifies in her Song of Praise <note>original: "Lof-sangh"; this refers to the biblical prayer found in 1 Samuel 2, where Hannah thanks God for the birth of her son</note> that the Lord makes poor and rich, and humbles and exalts. The truth of this is most clearly seen in the believers, whose condition is to be first humbled and afterwards exalted; they must first descend to Hell with their <term>Savior: "Salighmaker" in the original, meaning the one who brings salvation or bliss</term> before they can ascend with him to Heaven; they are not crowned before they have fought; they cannot reach the Land of Canaan <note>A biblical reference to the "Promised Land," often used here as a metaphor for Heaven or spiritual rest</note> except through a harsh wilderness. Their food

<meta>This page continues the dedication to Her Excellency (Amalia von Solms), moving from the general theological reasons for suffering to specific biblical precedents for deliverance. finally an exaltation comes; after a tearing, a healing; after blows, a binding <note>original: "naer ſlagen een verbindinge" — referring to the biblical image of God binding up wounds after a period of discipline</note>: weeping may endure for the length of the evening, but in the morning comes joy. Soon Noah was delivered from the <term>Flood: the biblical deluge from which Noah and his family were saved in the Ark</term>; Lot from Sodom; Jacob from Esau; Moses from Pharaoh; Israel from Egypt; David from Saul; the <term>three youths: Shadrach, Meshach, and Abednego, who were thrown into a furnace by King Nebuchadnezzar but were miraculously unharmed</term> from the fiery furnace; Daniel from the lions' den; Joseph from Herod; the Apostles from the Jews: and meanwhile, the Lord is around them with His Angels, with them through His promises, among them with His healing hand, and in them through the <term>Comforter: a traditional title for the Holy Spirit, translated from the Greek 'Paraclete'</term>, the Holy Spirit.

All of this, Your Excellency has truly experienced: for a short time it has pleased the Most High to humble Your Excellency through the hands of your and God’s enemies, who have pursued Your Excellency just as one might hunt a <term>partridge: a reference to 1 Samuel 26:20, where David describes himself as being hunted like a partridge in the mountains</term>. They have driven you from your Fatherland, made you an <term>Exile: a person living away from their native country, usually for political or religious reasons</term>, and sought to turn your life into death, your rest into unrest, your wealth into poverty, and your good fortune into misfortune;

<summary>The author lists several biblical figures who were delivered from hardship by God, asserting that the Lord remains present with the suffering. He then addresses the patroness directly, acknowledging her recent period of exile and persecution as a form of divine humbling.</summary>

all of which, and more besides, Your Excellency has been content to forsake for the Name of the Lord. So it is that Your Excellency may say with David; *You have snatched my Soul from death, my eyes from tears, my feet from slipping:* which Your Excellency Christianly acknowledges, as Your Excellency asks in her <term>Motto: a short phrase chosen to represent the guiding principles of a person or noble house</term>, *What shall I render unto the Lord? <note>original Latin: "Quid reddam Domino?" — a quote from Psalm 116:12</note> How shall I repay the Lord for all His benefits that He does for me?* The answer there

--- Page 16 ---
<meta>The previous page concluded the formal dedication by Maximiliaen Teellinck to Princess Amalia von Solms, dated February 1635. This page presents two commendatory poems: the first by the scholar Isaacus Gruterus, and the second addressed to the reader, likely praising the work of the late Willem Teellinck (Maximiliaen's father).</meta>

# On the New Jerusalem.

If the sanctuary of the Solymean <note>original: *Solymeeſch*. This refers to "Hierosolyma," the Latin name for Jerusalem, specifically evoking the glory of Solomon's Temple.</note> building
Lies smothered in ash; if through abiding grief
The sad corpse of God’s hallowed church
Is mourned; cease your sighing, you who see here, in this narrow space <note>This "narrow space" refers to the book itself, which contains the spirit of the author's teachings.</note>,
A spirit live again through pen and ink,
For the edifying of Jerusalem’s realm.
Yield, O Caesar’s hate, or the world’s fury;
From sulfur <note>Likely referring to the fires of war or the "brimstone" of destruction.</note> I set this cornerstone free,
Upon which, established, the hope of human salvation
Inherits God’s house, climbing beyond the reach of death’s day.

->ISAACUS GRUTERUS.<-

---

# To the World, and the Worldly-minded.

Open the doors of worldly hearts,
Learn to tear the yoke of sin;
Look, it is Teellinck <note>Willem Teellinck (1579–1629) was a pivotal figure in the Dutch Second Reformation (*Nadere Reformatie*). These poems celebrate the posthumous publication of his work.</note> who leads you,
Who prepares the way to God.
Have you ever caught soul-stirring words
(Heart-snares, cords for the senses)
Falling fiery from his mouth?
If you still long for that voice,
Here it begins to live again—
That which makes Satan’s kingdom tremble;
Which, sprinkled with heavenly dew,
Shows to man the treasure of piety.
Direct your conduct by this precept,
Bridle the desire for vanities,
Awaken both ear and heart to attention;
Christ provides a balm without pain.

->To each his own. <note>original: *Elck wacht het ſijne*. This was a common 17th-century motto, similar to the Latin "Suum cuique," suggesting that everyone will receive what is due to them, or should attend to their own duty.</note><-

<summary>This page contains two laudatory poems that introduce the book. They frame the work as a spiritual resurrection of the teachings of Willem Teellinck, intended to guide the reader toward piety and the "New Jerusalem."</summary>
<keywords>New Jerusalem, Isaacus Gruterus, Willem Teellinck, piety, Dutch Second Reformation, spiritual guidance, salvation</keywords>

--- Page 17 ---
<meta>The previous page concluded the introductory commendatory poems by Isaacus Gruterus and an anonymous author. This page, Folio 1, marks the beginning of the main text of the work by Willem Teellinck, titled "The New Jerusalem," which is structured as a devotional dialogue between Christ and Mary (representing the soul of the believer).</meta>

# T H E
## New Jerusalem.
### Presented
### In a dialogue between Christ and Mary, sitting at His feet.

### T H E F I R S T P A R T.
#### On the advantages of true Godliness <term>Godliness: *Godtsaligheyt* in Dutch, a central concept in the Dutch Second Reformation referring to a life of sincere, active piety and inner devotion.</term> / and what effect it brings about in the believer.

---

### The First Chapter.
Mary, having been visited by the Lord Christ, and sitting at Christ's feet, listens to His speech, and begins to speak with Him.

->M A R I A.<-

LORD Jesus / how sweet and friendly are Your words / Your lips are like dripping honeycomb / honey and milk are under Your tongue / and the scent of Your clothes is like the scent of Lebanon <note>This imagery is a direct reference to the Song of Solomon 4:11, commonly interpreted in this period as a metaphor for the relationship between Christ and the Church or the individual soul.</note> / Your mouth is a spring-

<summary>This page marks the beginning of Willem Teellinck's devotional dialogue, where the character of Mary (the believer) responds to Christ with poetic, biblical praise drawn from the Song of Solomon.</summary>
<keywords>New Jerusalem, dialogue, Christ and Mary, Godliness, piety, Song of Solomon, Dutch Second Reformation</keywords>

--- Page 18 ---
<meta>The previous page ended with Maria (the believer) praising Christ using imagery from the Song of Solomon, concluding with the word "spring-" (Dutch: fonteyne).</meta>

# The New Jerusalem.

-ing fountain of living waters <note>This continues the phrase from the previous page: "Your mouth is a spring-fountain of living waters," a common 17th-century metaphor for the spiritual nourishment found in Christ's words.</note>; Your words are pure truth and comfort; I cannot even express to You, Lord Jesus, what joy and solace it brings to my heart that You have deigned to come to me. Oh! how happy I would be if I might always stand before You, or sit at Your feet forever, and enjoy Your presence!

**The Lord.** Child, My physical presence is not of great benefit <note>This reflects a common theme in Reformed theology: that the spiritual presence of Christ through the Word and Spirit is more profitable for the believer than His physical presence on earth (referencing John 16:7).</note>, but it pleases Me well that you pay such good heed to My words; they are indeed the words of eternal life; if you press them firmly into your heart and rightly mingle them with true faith, you shall through them also come to live eternally: for although *all flesh is as grass, and all the glory of man as a flower of the field, which soon withers,* <margin>1 Peter 1:24</margin> yet My word remains for eternity; and that is the word that I proclaim to you.

**Mary.** I know, Lord, that it is so; I have become vividly aware of this in my heart, and my soul testifies it to me: for while You have spoken to Your handmaid <term>handmaid: *dienst-maeght*, a term of humble service often used by Mary, the mother of Jesus (Luke 1:38), adopted here by the soul of the believer.</term>, my Lord, my heart is ignited, my soul is ravished, and all my powers...

<summary>In this opening dialogue, Christ instructs the soul (Mary) that His spiritual Word is more vital than physical presence, citing the fleeting nature of life compared to the eternal Word. Mary responds with an ecstatic description of her heart being "ignited" by His speech.</summary>

### Chapter 2.

The Lord explains to Mary the advantages that a person gains from the <term>unfeigned: "ongeveynfde," meaning sincere or genuine</term> practice of <term>godliness: "Godtfaligheyt," a life characterized by piety and devotion to God</term>; first in this life.

<summary>In this passage, Mary confesses the ongoing struggle between her spiritual intentions and her physical desires, prompting the Lord to advise her on the necessity of self-denial. The text then transitions to a new chapter detailing the practical benefits of living a pious life.</summary>

### The New Jerusalem.

->The Lord.<-

To be more and more strengthened against those weaknesses, it is very useful and profitable that you thoroughly consider and weigh how the practice of <term>godliness: "Godtsaligheyt," a life of sincere piety and devotion to God</term> (even though it may feel somewhat troublesome to the <term>flesh: "vleesche," referring to human nature and its desires</term> for the present time, and seems to give birth to some fear and unrest) holds the promise of both the present and the future life: so that by practicing godliness in your life, in your death, and after your temporal death, you shall be happy always and eternally.

### The 3rd Chapter.
### The benefits which the truly Godly enjoy in their Death.

->The Lord.<-

**N**OW, the good that you, while practicing <term>godliness <note>original: "Godtsaligheyt." In this 17th-century context, this refers to a life of active, sincere piety and devotion to God's will.</note></term>, shall enjoy at the end of your life in death, consists in the perseverance of the aforementioned comfort: which commonly and faithfully accompanies all the godly in all their greatest distresses, and especially clings to them in death. For even though the wicked does not stand firm in his misfortune, by contrast the righteous is bold even in his death, so that you shall not look upon Death as a King of Terrors, **Job 18.14.** as he is to the wicked, but as a friendly messenger, sent out by your Heavenly Father to call you home to the possession of eternal joys.

**Maria.** How could that be, Lord, seeing that death is by nature so terrifying to all people?

**The Lord.** Thus, my child, when you begin the unfeigned <term>contemplation <note>original: "betracht-"; the word is completed by the catchword "trach-" on the next page to form "betrachtinge," meaning a deep, focused meditation or the practice of spiritual duties.</note></term>

<meta>catchword: contemplation</meta>

<summary>This page opens the third chapter, where the Lord explains to Mary that for those who live piously, death is transformed from a terrifying end into a "friendly messenger" that calls the soul back to God. It uses the biblical reference of Job to contrast the fear felt by the wicked with the confidence of the righteous.</summary>

### The New Jerusalem.
16

...where sorrow and pain shall have to depart. And so you shall, by reflecting well on this, be able to enjoy a rich comfort in that hour when you shall have need of your comfort; and you shall thus fall asleep blessedly in the body in the Lord, to awake in due time joyfully to eternal joys.

**Maria.** This is truly exceedingly comforting; but, Lord, the eye of my faith is very dim, so that I cannot see this well; and one also sees, dear Lord, sometimes, that even the **<term>godly <note>original: "Godtsalige," referring to those who live a life devoted to God's will and piety.</note></term>** themselves are quite oppressed <note>original: "beklemt," implying a sense of being smothered, anxious, or spiritually constricted.</note> in their dying.

**The Lord.** Even though some godly people sometimes have great anxieties in their dying (for the eye of their faith is somewhat troubled for that time), nevertheless their affairs stand right before God; and God’s **Angels** stand ready there (however little they may sometimes think so) to keep watch over their soul, and to receive it as soon as it departs from the body, and to lead it into **<term>Abraham’s bosom <note>original: "Abrahams schoot," a biblical reference to the place of peace and reward for the righteous after death, based on the parable in Luke 16:22.</note></term>**, yes, into **Heaven**, where there is fullness of joy, and a lovely existence always and forever: for it cannot fail; he who has lived godlily, dies blessedly.

<summary>In this passage, the Lord reassures Maria that even if a believer feels fear or spiritual darkness during death, their salvation is secure and angels are ready to escort them to heaven. The text emphasizes that a life lived in piety inevitably leads to a blessed death.</summary>
<keywords>comfort in death, Maria, The Lord, godliness, Abraham's bosom, angels, eternal joy, faith, salvation</keywords>

**M A R I A.** Oh! how darkened is poor man in his understanding regarding <term>spiritual things: "geestelijcke dingen"</term> / how he gropes for the wall blindly / how miserably he perceives himself in this / taking one thing for the other / and casting that out as a fruit of the true doctrine / which is solely a fruit of the contrary false doctrine. ->## The New Jerusalem.<-

**Tʜᴇ Lᴏʀᴅ.** This is also, my Friend, your misunderstanding and blindness; exactly the opposite is true: for when this holy doctrine—as is fitting—is well-presented to the people by my Ministers with proper discretion, then this holy doctrine serves very much to powerfully strike down and <term>humble: "verootmoedigen"</term> man; indeed, it powerfully works an excellent high esteem of God and a contempt for men: for when man comes to understand through this holy doctrine that it all depends on me, and that poor man is so <term>corrupt: "verdorven"</term> <note>This refers to the doctrine of "Total Depravity," the belief that human nature is so fallen that no one can choose God or do good without divine grace.</note> that he neither will nor can think anything good of himself or out of himself, much less do or perform it; as soon as he comes to some <term>apprehension: "apprehensie"</term>, powerful experience, and observation regarding this, his heart is thereby powerfully struck and wounded with terror, as if with the point of a diamond. <note>original: "met het punt van een Diamant." This is likely an allusion to Jeremiah 17:1, implying that the realization of sin is etched deeply and painfully into the heart.</note>

**Mᴀʀɪᴀ.** And how so then, Lord?

**Tʜᴇ Lᴏʀᴅ.** For through this doctrine he comes to see quite clearly that He alone can help him, whom he has constantly provoked every day of his <term>unconverted state: "onbekeerlijckheyt"</term> to destroy him: and therefore he cannot be at peace about it; but his heart begins [to]...

## The New Jerusalem

->## The New Jerusalem.<-

...then constantly to impress [upon him] that he must go out of himself, that he must go to God, and that he must cast himself upon His <term>mere, naked: "enckele bloote"</term> <note>This phrase emphasizes grace that is stripped of all human merit or secondary aids—grace and nothing else.</note> and entirely unmerited grace, and with <term>long-suffering: "lanckmoedigheyt"</term> wait for His appropriate time of help, if he is to be helped; and this also makes man pray quite earnestly and fervently, as if for life and limb, and cry out to God for grace, as seeing no other means of help, and now well-knowing that he has nothing in himself by which he might help himself.

Now this <term>agitation: "beroerte"</term>, restlessness, and anxiety serve tremendously to powerfully lead man toward true conversion, because through this he is brought into the state of <term>humiliation: "vernederinge"</term> and <term>contrition: "verslagentheyt"</term> <note>In the 17th century, "verslagentheyt" (contrition) described a heart "beaten down" or shattered by the realization of sin, a necessary step before receiving comfort.</note> where he must be, if he is to be converted: James 4:8, 9. <note>James 4:8-9: "Cleanse your hands, ye sinners... Be afflicted, and mourn, and weep: let your laughter be turned to mourning."</note> So that just as this holy doctrine is <term>solid food: "vaste spijse"</term> <note>A reference to Hebrews 5:14; the "solid food" of deeper doctrine is meant for the mature, yet here it is also used as a tool for the unrepentant.</note> for those who are already <term>born again: "weder-geboren"</term>, so it is likewise an <term>iron rod: "ysere roede"</term> <note>Referring to Psalm 2:9, "Thou shalt break them with a rod of iron." In this context, the doctrine acts as a tool to break the "potter's vessel" of human pride.</note> for the unregenerate, to drive them away from their vain trust in their own <term>ability: "vermogen"</term>, which is entirely hindering to them in the work of true conversion, and to make them cast themselves wholly and completely upon the mere, pure, unmerited grace of God.

the / <note>This continues the thought from the previous page: "not only many clever minds..."</note> but also those who are right well-meaning, and whom I believe also love You, yet they walk a different path and make a different profession than I do; so I find myself unsettled by this, and it leaves me in no small state of worry.

Know also that I shall yet open the eyes of those who belong to Me—even if they sometimes stray <note>original: "versuckelen" — to wander off, falter, or languish.</note> among the <term>flocks of my companions: "kudde mijner gesellen"</term> <note>A biblical allusion to the Song of Solomon 1:7, often interpreted in this period as a reference to different religious denominations or sects that claim to follow Christ but may lead the soul astray.</note>—in due time, and set them right. Thus it must not trouble you that there are so many various <term>persuasions: "gesintheden"</term> concerning the matter of Religion in the world: but this must comfort and confirm you: that you have received Me as a <term>Guide: "Leydts-man"</term> in this, for whom it is just as easy to show you the right way among a thousand side-paths, as if there were only one side-path next to the right—

<meta>Catchword: right- / rech-</meta>

<summary>The Lord comforts Maria’s anxiety over religious pluralism by explaining that her focus should remain on her own heart and God's personal guidance. He promises to eventually lead His true followers to the right path, regardless of the many confusing sects currently in the world.</summary>

## Chapter 9.

The Lord shows how, for the bringing forth of true **godliness**, true **humility**, right faith, and a holy intention are necessary.

->M A R I A.<-

**A**nd how must I then bring the doctrine of man’s <term>misery: "ellende"</term> <note>In the Reformed tradition, "misery" refers to the recognition of one's fallen state and sinfulness before God.</note> to my heart / in order to better bring forth true <term>godliness: "godtsaligheyt"</term> <note>This term refers to a life of active piety and devotion to God.</note> in my life?

->The LORD.<-

From the doctrine of man’s misery / you must learn true <term>humility: "ootmoedigheyt"</term> for yourself.

->M A R I A.<-

# The New Jerusalem 99

...the **Christian armor**? <note>This continues Maria's question from the previous page: "And what is that then, Lord—the Christian armor?"</note>

**The LORD.** The same consists of these six pieces: 1. **The belt of truth.** 2. **The breastplate of righteousness.** 3. **The shoes of the preparation of the Gospel of peace.** 4. **The shield of faith.** 5. **The helmet of salvation.** 6. **The sword of the Spirit.**

See, these are the **Christian armor** <term>Christian armor: Referring to the "Armor of God" from the biblical book of Ephesians, chapter 6, where spiritual virtues are compared to a soldier's equipment.</term>; and you must never consider yourself well-prepared or ready to appear before God or men, until you have put on all the parts of this Christian armor: for they are like your clothing, which you must guard well, so that you do not walk naked and your shame be not seen, **Revelation 16:15** <note>original: "Apoc.16.15." referring to the Apocalypse or Book of Revelation.</note>. Now, these are six in number.

**MARIA.** I shall see to doing so, Lord, through your help: but only, Lord, please explain this to me a bit more clearly, what each of them entails.

**The LORD.** 1. **The belt of truth** is the knowledge of Divine things, and your bounden duty toward them.

2. **The breastplate of righteousness** is a holy resolution to adapt and conform yourself to those known duties at all times, and as often as the occasion for it might arise.

# The New Jerusalem 100

3. **The shoes of the preparation of the Gospel of peace**, that is, a holy patience and perseverance to constantly proceed in the ways of God, in the practice of good works, regardless of what obstacles or hindrances one may encounter.

5. **The helmet of the hope of salvation**, that is, a holy longing and expectation for eternal salvation, which might sustain your heart in all adversities on earth.

6. **The sword of the Spirit**, that is, the right practice and handling of God's holy word, with which you can not only withstand Satan, but also cause him harm <note>original: "af-breucke doen," an old Dutch expression meaning to inflict damage, break down, or diminish the power of an enemy.</note>.

**MARIA.** Oh, Lord! Sad experience <note>ervarentheyt: Personal, lived experience, often implying lessons learned through trial and error.</note> has now taught me enough, to my own harm, that such a thing is of the highest necessity; but, Lord, how shall I best be able to do this?

# Chapter 2
## Of Continual Prayer

### THE LORD

Being well-armed and having kept good watch, you must still, continually feeling your own powerlessness, seek my help and assistance through arrow-prayers <note>uyt-schietende Gebeden: Short, spontaneous prayers "shot" toward heaven throughout the day, often called "ejaculatory prayers" in older devotional literature.</note>, so that you may acquit yourself well in the practice of **godliness** <term>Godtsaligheyt: Living a life of active piety and devotion to God.</term>: Matthew 26:41, Ephesians 6:17–18. For although you must by no means neglect to praise and pray to me in a formal manner <note>statelijcke wijse: Referring to organized, dignified, or scheduled prayer times, as opposed to spontaneous ones.</note> at certain hours of the day, Daniel 6:10, nevertheless, you must not be content with that alone, but rather consider that no time in the day should pass without the duty of prayer; because you must sanctify all your affairs through prayer, 1 Timothy 4:5. Take note, my friend: the gift of prayer is like the power of suckling <note>suygens: The natural instinct and strength of an infant to draw milk; here used as a metaphor for the soul drawing spiritual life from God.</note>, which the Lord, through the re-

6. ## The 4th Chapter.
### Of Christian Experience <term>ervarentheyt: In this context, it refers to "experimental" or "experiential" knowledge—faith that is felt and lived out through practice rather than just intellectually understood.</term>.

## The New Jerusalem.

you must also always see to add <note>original: "daer by te bougen" — literally "to bow" or "to bend toward," here meaning to attach or include in one's practice.</note> the practice of these three most Christian virtues: **Faith, Hope, and Love**; and to see that in all the conduct of your life, you might express completely and vividly the work of faith, the labor of love, and the patience of hope: ***1 Thessalonians 1:3.***

**MARIA.** Oh! **THE LORD.** You must see, my child, to mingle your conduct with faith, if it is to be pleasing to me: ***Romans 14:23-24.*** <note>While modern Bibles end Romans 14 at verse 23, some older traditions included the concluding doxology here or referred to the transition into the next chapter.</note> For even the observance of the most holy ordinances of God—such as Prayer, ***James 1:6***, and the Word, ***Hebrews 4:2***—do not please God unless they are mingled with faith: so you may easily conclude from this, that many other common and humble works <note>original: "slechte en geringe wercken" — in this period, "slecht" meant "simple" or "plain" rather than "evil."</note> must be adorned with faith, if they are to be pleasing to me.

In this consideration, beyond the general rule—namely, that you do unto him just as you would have others do unto you: **Matthew 7:12.** <note>The "Golden Rule": "Therefore all things whatsoever ye would that men should do to you, do ye even so to them."</note> First, that in all you do, you seek not only what is your own, but also that which belongs to another: **Philippians 2:4.** <note>“Look not every man on his own things, but every man also on the things of others.”</note> And therefore you must often, as circumstances arise, set aside your own advantage to do good to others and to prevent another's sorrow.

# The New Jerusalem. 140

...and look upon my <term>merits <gloss>original: "verdiensten" — the theological concept that Christ's perfect life and sacrifice earned the "merit" required for salvation, which is then credited to the believer.</gloss></term> and complete <term>satisfaction <gloss>original: "genoeghdoeninge" — a legal-theological term meaning the payment of a debt or the "satisfying" of a penalty required by justice.</gloss></term>; see, this is your failing and foolishness: or do you think you can ever find in yourself that which might reconcile you to the justice of God? Do you wish to seek your salvation in yourself, or do you think to be your own <term>Savior <gloss>original: "Salighmakerse" — the feminine form of "Savior," used here because Maria represents the soul, which was traditionally gendered female in spiritual literature.</gloss></term>? And to bind the Lord your God to you through your own worthiness and virtue, so that he would be obliged to give you Heaven? 

How! Do you not consider that your own deeds condemn you, but that it is my merits that must save you? Or do you think that my merits are of no value, and that my bitter suffering has become powerless? Can you think that your baseness and unworthiness is so great that my merits and worthiness could not cover them, and your sins so deadly that my blood could not heal them? Have I not come into the world to save that which was lost? <note>Luke 19:10: "For the Son of man is come to seek and to save that which was lost."</note> To call, not the righteous, but sinners to <term>repentance <gloss>original: "bekeeringe" — literally "turning toward," signifying a total change of heart and direction.</gloss></term>? <note>Matthew 9:13.</note> Has not my Heavenly Father also testified of me, that he is well-pleased in me? <note>Matthew 3:17.</note> And that in me, the <term>Beloved <gloss>original: "den Beminden" — a title for Christ used in the Epistles.</gloss></term>, he holds acceptable all sinners who receive me? <note>Ephesians 1:5, 11.</note>

<sig>MA-</sig>

<summary>The Lord reproves Maria for her doubt, explaining that salvation depends entirely on Christ’s merits and sacrifice rather than her own worthiness or deeds. He reminds her that he came specifically for sinners and that God the Father accepts all who receive the Son.</summary>

# The New Jerusalem. 141

MARIA. Far be it from me, Lord, that I should doubt the worthiness or efficacy of your <term>merits <gloss>original: "verdiensten" — the theological concept that Christ's perfect obedience and suffering earned the "credit" required to satisfy God's justice.</gloss></term>; I know it well, Lord, I indeed know it well, and am through your <term>grace <gloss>original: "genade" — unmerited favor or divine assistance.</gloss></term> fully convinced in my heart that you are a complete **Savior** <gloss>original: "Salighmaker" — literally "blessed-maker."</gloss>, powerful to save to the uttermost all those who receive you. But this is what keeps me troubled, Lord, and makes me doubt: that I have not conducted myself well before you, that I have not received you well, that I do not rely upon you, that I do not love you, and that I do not fear and serve you as the <term>true believers <gloss>original: "recht-geloovige" — those who possess the correct, living faith as opposed to a merely intellectual one.</gloss></term> are accustomed to doing. 

Could I but perceive a continuous zeal in the reading of your Word, in prayer, and in other holy <term>exercises <gloss>original: "oeffeningen" — spiritual disciplines like fasting or meditation intended to train the soul.</gloss></term> and works of love, then I would dare to take courage and somewhat assure myself that you wish to credit your merits to me for my salvation. But since I all too often find myself so completely faint and lacking zeal in your holy things, my heart fails me; and I can hardly trust that you would still wish to grant your most worthy and precious merits to such a base and unworthy person as I am, and cause them to take root in my heart.

<sig>G 4</sig>

<summary>Maria responds to the Lord by acknowledging the power of his sacrifice but expressing her deep-seated anxiety that her own lack of religious zeal and spiritual discipline makes her unworthy to receive his grace.</summary>

<keywords>Maria, Savior, merits, grace, spiritual exercises, zeal, doubt, unworthiness, salvation, prayer, Word of God</keywords>

# The New Jerusalem. 142

**The Lord.** My Pious One! Mark what I say: you must watch yourself very closely here, for the path here is slippery; if you would wish to avoid that cursed <term>conceit <gloss>original: "verwaentheyt" — spiritual pride or the delusion of one's own self-sufficiency.</gloss></term> through which thousands of ill-advised and boldly presumptuous people go to ruin, falling into a false confidence in their own doing.

**Maria.** Oh! Lord, protect me from that by your good Spirit; how could such a thing happen?

**The Lord.** Namely, if you should take heart regarding the assurance of your <term>salvation <gloss>original: "saligheyt" — the state of being saved or blessedness.</gloss></term>, not because you have me as your **Savior** and **Advocate** <gloss>original: "Voor-spreeker" — one who speaks on behalf of another, a legal intercessor.</gloss>, but solely because you yourself performed your duties finely and well according to your own mind in my service. 

And so it often goes with many of my "little ones": when they have done their tasks finely and well in the service of God, then they have good courage and a good confidence in God. That is well; but on the other hand, when they cannot <term>carry out <gloss>original: "uyt-voeren" — to execute or perform a task to completion.</gloss></term> their affairs so well (even though they do their best), then their courage falters <note>literally "lies down"</note>. Now, that I do not praise; for I am not only the advocate of the believers when they can perform their affairs reasonably well, but also when they...

<meta>catchword: through</meta>

<summary>The Lord warns Maria that basing her spiritual confidence on her own performance is a form of pride, explaining that his role as Advocate remains constant even when a believer's zeal falters.</summary>

# The New Jerusalem. 143

...through pressing temptations or are otherwise bound in great weakness and deficiency in the <term>carrying out <gloss>original: "uyt-voeren" — the performance or execution of spiritual duties.</gloss></term> of their affairs.

**Maria.** Oh! Lord, I well understand that your grace and <term>loving-kindness <gloss>original: "goedertierentheydt" — a traditional term for God’s merciful and loyal love.</gloss></term> toward your children is a thousand times greater than my narrow heart can comprehend. But, Lord, will not such a teaching open a door to sloppy negligence and <term>easy-going <gloss>original: "soetvoerige" — literally 'sweet-moving'; describing a complacency that feels pleasant or "sweet" but leads to spiritual laziness.</gloss></term> carelessness in your most holy service? For you alone are worthy to be honored and served with the greatest alertness and the best care in the world.

**The Lord.** Oh no, my Pious One; for you must observe that I am not speaking here of those who, through an adopted easy-going carelessness, continually perform and attend to the things of **God** quite sloppily—doing so more out of habit to keep up appearances than out of any reverence or longing for **God**. Rather, I am speaking here of those who, throughout the course of their lives, sincerely strive and endeavor to do the things of **God** well. And of such people, I say that it is their weakness and fault that they do not take heart in my **merits** <note>The "merits of Christ" refers to the theological belief that Jesus's perfect life and sacrifice provide the "credit" for a believer's standing before God, rather than the believer's own works.</note> just as much as they...

<meta>catchword: but</meta>

<summary>Maria worries that emphasizing God's grace might encourage laziness, but the Lord explains that his comfort is intended for sincere, struggling believers, not for those who are habitually negligent or indifferent.</summary>

# The New Jerusalem. 144

...but [feel they] have performed their duties poorly, [any more] than when they have done them with great zeal. For indeed, my **merits** <note>Referring to the "merits of Christ," the theological concept that Jesus’s perfect righteousness is credited to the believer.</note> stand to their benefit, not only when they do their things very excellently, but also when they are found to be very weak and deficient in their actions. And it is so far from being the case that this—as you supposed—would open a door to sloppy negligence and <term>easy-going carelessness <gloss>original: "soet-voerige sorgeloosheydt" — a comfortable, unconcerned state of spiritual stagnation.</gloss></term> in the service of **God**: on the contrary, it is of great and inexpressible <term>benefit <gloss>original: "nuttighept" — spiritual utility or profit.</gloss></term> for making all <term>sincere hearts <gloss>original: "oprechte herten" — those whose inner intentions are truly focused on God.</gloss></term> (for it is of such people I now speak) more and more awake, and ready, willing, and also earnest, and consequently also <term>zealous <gloss>original: "yverigh" — fervent and diligent in devotion.</gloss></term> in all <term>holy contemplations <gloss>original: "heylige betrachtingen" — spiritual exercises, meditations, or religious duties.</gloss></term>. For experience incontrovertibly teaches that there is nothing in the world that makes sincere hearts slacken more in all holy contemplations—indeed, that more often causes them to neglect them or perform them quite poorly—than this: that they feel their actions and contemplations are not at all acceptable to **God**, and that the **Lord** indeed has no pleasure in them, nor can take any. This causes their courage to fail them entirely; then, all their strength lies down upon the earth, and so...

<meta>catchword: and</meta>

<summary>The Lord explains that his grace does not encourage negligence; rather, knowing that God accepts even weak efforts prevents sincere believers from losing heart and giving up entirely due to feelings of inadequacy.</summary>

# The New Jerusalem. 145

...and there is no penetrating lifting up of the heart toward **Heaven** to **God**. With a fresh, new zeal, they would then begin to take up and carry out the things of **God** in good time. For we certainly know this: nothing in the world makes us take a matter of great importance in hand and carry it out with more <term>alertness <gloss>original: "wackerheyt" — a state of being spiritually awake, diligent, and vigilant.</gloss></term> than when we are well-assured that the matter will succeed and that much good will come from it. Such a conviction will make a poor patient willingly drink down even the <note>original: "vileynsten dranck" — the vilest-tasting drink; a metaphor for the bitter medicine of difficult duties or trials.</note> vilest-tasting medicine that can be put to his lips.

**MARIA.** Truly, this is very <term>striking <gloss>original: "treffelijck" — remarkable or excellent.</gloss></term>, **Lord**; and I must confess that I have been very deficient in this until now. May the good **God** forgive me out of grace, and I thank you...

# The New Jerusalem. 146

**Lord**, that you still give me such understanding: I hope I shall, through **God’s** grace, henceforth <note>original: "beter daer in quÿten" — better acquit or conduct myself in this regard.</note> do better in this.

**THE LORD.** Yes, my <term>Pious One <gloss>original: "Vrome" — a term for a devout or godly person, often used affectionately in this text.</gloss></term>; all people are by nature very much inclined to build their comfort upon their own doing. Therefore, it is no wonder that so many who are called **Christians** are found who still partly seek their <term>righteousness <gloss>original: "rechtveerdigheyt" — the theological state of being justified or made right before God.</gloss></term> in their works. People are naturally inclined toward this, even though they are restrained by sound doctrine so that they do not openly promote or dare to accept such a thing <note>This refers to the "sneaky" nature of legalism—even when people know they are saved by grace, they still secretly try to earn God's favor through good deeds.</note>.

**MARIA.** Oh! Yes, my **Lord**; I acknowledge that the work of **God’s** grace, wrought in us with power <note>References to Colossians 1:29 and 2 Thessalonians 1:11.</note>, can and ought to greatly comfort us in the sweet feeling of undeserved grace and the friendliness of **God** toward us.

**THE LORD.** That is not what I am rebuking here. Rather, it is this: that while you take courage and rest easy before **God** whenever you have been able to perform the aforementioned <term>exercises <gloss>original: "oeffeningen" — spiritual disciplines such as prayer, meditation, or reading scripture.</gloss></term> with a <note>original: "puer" — purity or clarity of mind.</note> pure spirit and with <term>fervor <gloss>original: "vÿerigheyt" — literally "fieriness"; a state of intense spiritual passion or zeal.</gloss></term>; you, on the contrary, easily let your courage fall completely when that <note>original: "gevoeligen puyr" — the "sensible" or tangible feeling of purity and spiritual clarity.</note> tangible purity leaves you during those holy exercises.

### Of the growth in true godliness.

**Sixthly**, that you strive from day to day to learn more and more of the things of God revealed in my <term>Word — "Woordt," the Holy Scriptures.</term>; you cannot increase in spiritual gifts if you do not increase in the <term>knowledge — "kennisse," implying a deep, experiential understanding of divine truth.</term> of spiritual things:

Such things I know, I who know their hearts. All their time they spent <term>profitably — "profijtelijck," meaning spiritually useful or for the benefit of their souls.</term>, and when they occupied themselves with God, time was always too short for them; and through the great <term>sweetness — "soetigheden," a common mystical term for the pleasant, comforting spiritual sensations felt during deep prayer.</term> of devotion which they felt in their **contemplation**, they sometimes even forgot to give their bodies their necessary food or drink. They withdrew from and abandoned all riches, high status, and honors, and all their friends and kinsfolk of this world. Thus they were poor in earthly goods, but very rich in divine graces and **virtues**; and they were strangers to the world, but very well known to God, as fine and intimate friends. They considered themselves as nothing, but in the eyes of God they were precious and **chosen**: they remained always in great <term>humility — "ootmoedigheyt," the foundational virtue of recognizing one's own nothingness before God.</term>; they lived in pure obedience; they walked in love and patience. Therefore, they increased daily in the Spirit and in a spiritual <term>disposition — "gemoedt," the inner state of the mind or soul.</term>, and they obtained very great grace with God. They have been exalted by me and set as an **example**: therefore you should rightly be drawn more and more by their examples to increase in virtues, rather than by the multitude of <term>lukewarm — "lauwe flauwe," a reference to those who are indifferent or lacking in spiritual zeal, often echoing the biblical warning against being "neither hot nor cold."</term> and faint-hearted people,

# T  A  B  L  E   O  F   C  O  N  T  E  N  T  S .

Chapter 4. Of Christian experience. <note>In the context of the Dutch Further Reformation, "experience" (ervarentheyt) refers to the personal, felt reality of God's work in the believer's soul, distinguishing it from mere head-knowledge.</note> page: 119.

Chapter 5. Of Faith, Hope, and Love. page: 127.

Chapter 6. Of the assurance of salvation. <note>The "assurance of salvation" was a central theme for Teellinck, addressing the believer's search for certainty regarding their standing with God.</note> page: 138.

Chapter 7. Of growth in true Godliness. <note>"Godliness" (Godt-saligheyt) is the hallmark of Teellinck’s theology, emphasizing a life of active piety and devotion to God.</note> page: 165.

# Paul’s Complaint, <note>original: "De klachte Pauli." Teellinck uses the Latin genitive form of Paul’s name, a common scholarly convention of the time.</note>
## Concerning his natural
## corruption: with the me-
## thod to be delivered
## from the power thereof.

->Presented in two Sermons, from<-
->Romans 7 verse 24.<- <note>This verse is a cornerstone of Reformed theology, where the Apostle Paul laments his ongoing struggle with sin despite his desire to do good.</note>

->Wretched man that I am! who shall de-<-
->liver me from this body of death.<-

->By<-

# WILLEM TEELLINCK,
->Minister of the Holy Gospel with-<-
->in Middelburg.<- <note>Teellinck (1579–1629) was a central figure in the "Nadere Reformatie" (Dutch Further Reformation), a movement that emphasized personal piety and the inner spiritual life over mere formal doctrine.</note>

### At Dordrecht,

->Printed for François Boels, re-<-
->siding by the Town Hall, in the white crowned<-
->Dove. In the Year 1651.<- <note>In the 17th century, houses and shops were identified by signs like "The White Crowned Dove" rather than street numbers.</note>
